        Appendices Appendix C: Using the Ecosystem Integrity Five-star System




C.4 Assessment Strategies

C.4.1 Preparation

Before assessing any of the 21 sub-attributes, a series of
recommended steps can be taken to ensure a systematic and
rigorous evaluation of the site.

1. Select random sampling locations to evaluate sub-attributes.
   Plots, transects and points are useful for long-term monitoring
   and assessment because they can be used consistently and also
   during re-evaluation of the site.
2. Develop a site sampling plan and analysis methodology. Based
   on agreed indicators, and a deﬁned value for each star, the TGBS
   Five-star System’s sub-attributes can be evaluated using a variety
   of cost-effective and time-efﬁcient methods.
3. Determine the quantitative metrics to be used to monitor
   indicators for each sub-attribute (Appendix F).
4. Set the highest value (5 stars) to the lowest value (0 stars) for
   each sub-attribute based on the selected indicators and metrics
   for the reference model.
5. Collect data directly from sampling sites or through subsequent
   analysis.
6. Score each sub-attribute for which data are available and average
   available values for each attribute.
7. Ensure methods, raw data and analyses are stored, curated and        G-team Bino bird observations. (TRCRC)
   available for future resampling or historical documentation.
                                                                        Tips:
                                                                        • Where appropriate, use methods that allow for the collection of
                                                                          evidence for multiple sub-attributes (section 5.5.1).
                                                                        • Work with local and subject specialists to develop data collection
                                                                          and interpretation methodologies.
                                                                        • Utilise multipurpose instruments to save time and cost. For
                                                                          example, some soil test kits measure soil pH, nutrient levels,
                                                                          organic matter content and certain heavy metals, contributing
                                                                          data for multiple sub-attributes (e.g., contamination, substrate
                                                                          chemical conditions and substrate physical conditions).
                                                                        • Use calibrated equipment, follow sampling protocols, and measure
                                                                          sub-attribute indicators accurately.



                                                                        C.5 Techniques

                                                                        The assessment of ecosystem integrity is a complex process and
                                                                        there is no one-size-ﬁts-all approach. Evidence and data collection
                                                                        methods for all 21 sub-attributes have been compiled below to help
                                                                        assessors make informed decisions about methods tailored to ﬁt the
                                                                        unique characteristics of each site.

topography) required to sustain the ecosystem are present.

Appropriate levels of growth and productivity, nutrient cycling,
decomposition, habitat, species interactions, and types and rates of
natural disturbance regimes are present.

C.6 Rating Procedure                                                    • Attachment: Refers to any additional supporting documents,
                                                                          ﬁles, or references that complement or provide more in-depth
The rating procedure shall be done by assessors after collecting data     information about the assessment. Attachments enhance
on sub-attributes based on measurable indicators, appropriate             transparency and allow stakeholders to delve deeper into the
methods and tools, and ensuring consistency and accuracy in               assessment details.
assessments. The rating procedure compares the site condition to
the reference condition deﬁned using the Ecosystem Integrity Five-
star System (section C.7, Appendix F). Rating points indicate           C.7 Developing the Reference Model
whether sub-attributes have improved or degraded.

In the ﬁnal assessment of criteria 1 and 4-7 (Appendix B.7), the        TGBS assessment, it is necessary to deﬁne speciﬁc indicators and
assessment ratings are recorded based on results of the remote          metrics appropriate to the project and describe the reference model
sensing and ﬁeld survey reports (Appendices B.4 and B.5), and           for each project by calibrating the Five-star System (section 2.5,
changes in each sub-attribute are scored.                               Appendix F). The reference model or condition is described by the
                                                                        ﬁve-star value for each sub-attribute.

C.8 Visualise Ecosystem Integrity Change                                                                                                 Each key attribute is rated or scored using a ﬁve-star scale. Stars
                                                                                                                                         indicate attribute condition at the time of measurement.
The Ecological Recovery Wheel (ERW) is a tool for conveying
progress of recovery of ecosystem attributes compared to those of                                                                        Practitioners and stakeholders can assess biodiversity and
a reference model (Gann et al. 2019; Figure C.7). It is a circular                                                                       ecosystem integrity through the TGBS assessment process and then
diagram with sections representing the ecosystem through six key                                                                         visually display the result of these attribute ratings on the ERW
attributes and 21 sub-attributes. The wheel shows the ecosystem’s                                                                        (Figure B1). Displaying multiple wheels (e.g., baseline versus current
progress towards a desired state.                                                                                                        condition), the ERW can be used to track changes, identify areas for
                                                                                                                                         improvement, and guide restoration decisions. It can help
The ERW has major sections for the key ecosystem attributes of                                                                           stakeholders visualise complex ecosystem attribute interactions and
absence of threats, physical conditions, species composition,                                                                            promote holistic ecological recovery.
structural diversity, ecosystem function and external exchanges.

Appendix D: Remote Sensing Assessment Methods




This appendix provides a detailed description of the remote sensing           is designed to complement the ﬁeld-based survey methods to
assessment methods (RSAM) that were developed to evaluate the                 provide additional information on the biodiversity values and threats
sub-attributes of the Five-star System of the Global Biodiversity             at each site and support TGBS assessments. The RSAM can be
Standard (TGBS). The RSAM is based on the analysis of satellite               applied at different scales to the sites, depending on the availability
imagery and geospatial data to measure indicators of ecosystem                and resolution of the data sources. The RSAM is also useful to help
integrity, including landscape connectivity, fragmentation, and the           deﬁne the baseline and develop the reference ecosystem as a
presence and distribution of threatened and rare species. The RSAM            comparison to ﬁeld survey assessments.

process, assessors shall consider the following:                            • Identifying and approaching elderly people may be easy but
                                                                              sometimes they may not be able to clearly communicate because
• Does the assessment team have the requisite language(s) skills              of (deteriorating) health conditions, memory loss and lack of
  and knowledge of the cultural context to engage with local                  information. A low elderly population in a village may also lead to
  stakeholders effectively? This includes knowledge of gender and             bias if data is only collected from elderly people.
  sociocultural norms, a gender-balanced assessment team, if
  necessary, and the ability to communicate in the language most            Indigenous peoples (IPs)
  commonly used by the stakeholder group.                                   • IPs self-identify as indigenous, and generally have a historical
• Do education, social hierarchy, literacy and language differences           continuity with a given region prior to colonisation and a strong
  mean that local communities may not feel comfortable being open             link to their lands. They maintain, at least in part, distinct social,

E.3 Facilitation and Communication Skills

Assessors shall carry out interviews and/or focus group discussions         E.4 Semi-structured Interview 13
with local stakeholders (section 5.7), and facilitating these effectively
requires some good communication skills.                                    Semi-structured interviews are an effective information-gathering
                                                                            tool for assessing social aspects of restoration projects.
When communicating, assessors shall:
• Be open-minded and ready to modify preconceptions                         E.4.1 When to Use this Tool
• Speak clearly and slowly, using the language most commonly
  used by the stakeholder group                                             Semi-structured interviews are very useful during TGBS assessments
• Smile and make eye contact                                                when the assessor needs to interact with a particular stakeholder
• Have open body language (e.g., do not have crossed arms)                  group (particularly an identiﬁed disadvantaged group), or with a
• Ask non-leading questions, listen to answers, and seek                    knowledgeable person, but would like to do this away from other
  conﬁrmation and clariﬁcation as required                                  stakeholders or the project team because of the need to discuss
                                                                            sensitive issues in private.
Facilitating group discussions
When assessors are engaging with groups of stakeholders (for                E.4.2 What Is the Purpose of a Semi-structured
example, during a focus group discussion), they shall make sure that        Interview?

contribute while respecting existing hierarchies. Assessors shall also      Semi-structured interviews:

   the ground). Normally a semi-structured interview shall not take
   more than about 30 minutes. For reasons of safeguarding, the
   interviewers should work in pairs: one person can take notes
   and the other to guide the dialogue.
4. Begin by explaining the purpose of the semi-structured interview
   to the person or group participating. Explain that, if necessary, all
   information provided will be treated conﬁdentially. If you wish to
   record the discussion (this is not usually recommended), then
   make sure you have everyone’s permission to do so.
5. Ask a few prepared formal introductory questions (see point 1
   above). Make sure that in a group everyone has spoken – if only
   to introduce themselves.
6. Work through the checklist you prepared earlier by asking
   relevant questions and carefully listening to the answers.

E.4.6 Examples of Closed/Open and Leading Questions                      E.5 Focus Group Discussion14
for Semi-structured Interviews
                                                                         Focus group discussions are another effective information-gathering
                                                                         tool for assessing social aspects of restoration projects.
   Don’t ask:
                                                                         E.5.1 What Is the Purpose of a Focus Group
                                                                         Discussion?
   ‘Is the project going well?’ or ‘Are there any problems with
   the project?’ or ‘Have you beneﬁted from the project?’                A focus group discussion is a way to bring together several
                                                                         members of a group having a common interest or common identity.
                                                                         The purpose of a focus group is to understand in greater depth a
                                                                         particular topic or issue that is relevant to the project. It differs from
   Do ask:                                                               a wider group because of the focus group’s make-up that ensures
                                                                         that everyone participating has something relevant to contribute to
                                                                         the discussion.
   ‘How do you feel about the project so far?’ then, based on the
   answer given, ask follow-up questions to get more detail.             E.5.2 Why Is this Tool Useful?

                                                                         A focus group discussion is a useful tool to engage with a
                                                                         disadvantaged group (e.g., women, minority ethnic groups, children)
                                                                         as they may ﬁnd it less intimidating to discuss things in a closed
                                                                         group of known people.
   Don’t ask:
                                                                         Group discussions are a useful way to obtain several perspectives
                                                                         about a project, activity or other topic. Unlike one-to-one interviews,
   ‘Do you collect medicinal plants from the forest?’                    members in a group can build on each other’s responses and come
                                                                         up with ideas that they may not have thought of by themselves.
                                                                         Group discussions are particularly beneﬁcial for bringing together a
                                                                         range of people or stakeholders (e.g., staff, students, local
   Do ask:                                                               authorities, communities, local businesses).

                                                                         E.5.3 Who Should Participate?
   ‘What sort of resources do you collect from the forest?’ then
   follow up (probe more deeply) by asking about each different          Ideally 8-10 participants and a facilitator and a note taker. It can be
   plant that has been mentioned in turn, e.g., ‘What is it being        useful to have separate focus groups so each one can accommodate
   collected for?’, ‘Who collects it?’, ‘How abundant is it?’ etc.       participants of a similar social or other background.

                                                                         E.5.4 How to Use this Tool

                                                                         Before starting, prepare a topic guide, which is a rough list of
                                                                         questions divided into sub-set categories (or themes). The purpose
   Don’t ask:                                                            of this is to loosely structure the focus group discussion and to guide
                                                                         the group’s discussion (as naturally as possible) through the topics.
                                                                         It is good practice to record the focus groups (but remember to get
   ‘Is the forest now more degraded than it used to be?’                 participants’ consent if doing this), so that the discussions can be
   Or ‘Is there any illegal logging taking place in the forest?’         transcribed word for word. This allows for easier analysis and serves
                                                                         as a written record of the session. Questions for the group shall be
                                                                         qualitative, unbiased and open.

   Do ask:                                                               1. At the start of the focus group discussion, welcome the group and
                                                                            introduce yourself and your team. Check that all participants
                                                                            understand the conﬁdentiality policy and risks. Cover any
   ‘How does the condition of the forest now compare with                   housekeeping notes and then begin by explaining the purpose of
   what it used to be?’ then follow up (probe) by asking: ‘What             the focus group discussion and the objective for the session (be
   do you think are the causes of the changes in forest                     careful not to give too much away as it could bias the responses).
   condition?’                                                              At this point it is generally a good idea to pause for any questions
                                                                            that the group may have. Think about how best the group should
                                                                            be seated so as to promote equality.

2. Set out some basic ground rules and remind participants that:         E.5.5 Example Questions for Focus Groups Discussions15
   - You are interested in their responses and experiences
   - They should speak one at a time (for the audio recorder or note     The questions posed during focus groups serve as the agenda for the
      taker)                                                             group discussion. A good question will stimulate good interaction
   - You want to hear everyone’s views                                   among group participants. Some questions have the potential to
   - They should listen to each other and respect each other’s views     exclude certain points of view through false assumptions or narrow,
   - You might ask them to move on to a question or revisit a            inappropriately phrased and poorly designed questions, which can
      question depending on the time available                           affect the quality of the information provided.
   - Any views or opinions expressed during the focus group will
      be conﬁdential and anonymised                                      To understand who beneﬁts from the project, and how, the following
3. Begin with an icebreaker to get participants comfortable with         questions may be asked:
   speaking, and establish the moderator as the leader of the group
   (allow approximately 10 minutes for this).                            • How did a particular project intervention or policy impact you?
4. During the discussion, encourage the conversation (through            • Did it lead to a change in income and/or employment
   prompts) and allow the group to lead their own discussion. If the       opportunities?
   dynamics of the group work well, you will be able to do and say       • Who was mainly employed (male or female participants, lower
   less, keep listening and let the group naturally interact over the      class, migrant workers, labourers)?
   topic. In these circumstances, keep an eye on the clock and only      • What differences did you notice in the surrounding environment?
   intervene when you have heard suitable responses and would            • Did you notice changes in water quality, soil fertility, emergence
   like to move on.                                                        of butterﬂies, etc.?
5. Occasionally, the group’s dynamics can be unhelpful for the           • How was income from the project used within the family?
   stimulation of a discussion, particularly where there are breakaway   • Were you able to access health and educational beneﬁts with
   conversations among two or more individuals or where there is           increased income?
   tension in the group. In these circumstances there are a number of    • Is there anything you’d like to ask me?
   things you can do to get the group back on track:                     • Is there anything I haven’t asked that you think I should have?
   - Challenge and close down dominant characters (politely at ﬁrst)
   - If a participant makes persistent negative remarks, remind
      participants of the house rules to respect one another and of
      the fact that this is a safe space
   - Give quieter participants a chance to contribute (look out for
      signs that they have something to say but avoid putting them
      on the spot)
   - Diffuse conﬂicts by moving the topic on (or parking an issue)
   - Move into a more creative mode (for example, get the group up
      and ask them to engage in an exercise before sitting down in
      different seats)
   - Introduce an activity to the group that requires them to work
      among themselves (this can also give the moderator a break)
6. At the end of the focus group, summarise the important things
   you have learned from the focus group and reﬂect on some of the
   emerging issues. Remember to thank the participants for their
   time and participation, and to let them know that you appreciate      A focal group discussion about conservation planning with local people
   their contributions. Explain to them the next steps and share how     at Ankafobe, Madagascar. (Missouri Botanical Garden Madagascar)
   the information from the focus group discussion will be used.

        Appendices Appendix H: Stakeholder Engagement and Social Beneﬁts Ratings




Appendix H: Stakeholder Engagement and Social
Beneﬁts Ratings



   Contents                                                                           Signiﬁcant involvement of stakeholders in project activities could
                                                                                      involve stakeholders taking part in several activities, stakeholders
                                                                                      having ongoing involvement in project activities rather than a one-off
   H.1 Stakeholder Engagement . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . .170              (e.g., ongoing project monitoring rather than a one-off tree planting
                                                                                      day) or signiﬁcant involvement in one activity (e.g., stakeholders
   H.2 Beneﬁts Distribution . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . .172        carrying out participatory mapping, and the resulting map being used
                                                                                      to establish formal access and use rights for stakeholders).
   H.3 Knowledge Enrichment . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . .173

   H.4 Sustainable Economies . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . .173




H.1 Stakeholder Engagement

A maximum of 4 points is available for scoring criterion 3 attribute
1: stakeholder engagement.

+1 point: Awarded for stakeholders being identiﬁed and made
aware of the project and rationale. This can be evidenced through
the project having completed a stakeholder analysis or mapping
exercise, and the applicant creating awareness of the project
through distributing ﬂyers, holding community meetings, and
establishing and communicating feedback and grievance
mechanisms.

+1 point: Awarded when the applicant shows that a grievance
mechanism exists and is accessible to stakeholders. This can be
evidenced through documentation, and through conﬁrming with
stakeholders that they are aware of the grievance process.

+1 point: Awarded when the applicant shows that primary
stakeholders are involved and support the project.
Involvement should increase from the start of implementation.

+1 point: Awarded when the applicant shows that secondary
stakeholders are involved and support the project.
Involvement should increase from the start of implementation.

For medium to large projects (>500 ha), it would be expected that                     Local communities can beneﬁt from employment in native tree
the project demonstrates a high level of involvement of primary and                   nurseries, such as this one in Agnalazaha Forest, Madagascar.
secondary stakeholders to earn the respective points.                                 (Missouri Botanical Garden Madagascar)
